974. JOHN MEAD , Robbery, with violence on William Woods, and stealing a purse and 16 s., his goods and money.
MR. KEITH FRITH Prosecuted.
WILLIAM WOODS . I live at Pentonville Chambers, Pentonville Road, and am a coach painter—about half-past 12 on Sunday morning, 31st August, I was in the New Cut, when the prisoner, whom I see nightly in my sleep, caught me by my coat collar with one hand, and by the throat with the other, and ran me up against the shutters—there were three men with him, besides the one that carried my carpet bag—when against the shutters they pinned my arms and held my arms; I tried to get rid of the choking, I could hardly breathe—the prisoner rifled my pockets and turned them inside out, and he kept his grip on me till I fell down—they took my purse, 16 s. or more, and some tobacco, and then they left me and ran away—I was taken to a lodging near by—a policeman came to me after that—next morning I identified the prisoner from several other persons—the prisoner used the violence to my throat, I suffered a great deal of pain—I was not able to speak for some hours afterwards—I have been spitting blood ever since, and am still suffering from the effects—I am 73 years old.
Cross-examined. I am sure you are the man—you nearly strangled me—I could not swear to the clothes you had on—I know your features.
By the COURT. I first saw him at a lamp-post with three other respectable looking men, but the man that had my carpet bag belonged to the school, and led me into the lion's den.
ALFRED WARD . From information I received, early on Sunday morning, 31st August, I made inquiries, and found the prisoner in a house in Langfranc Street, Waterloo Road, drinking with a number of others—I knew him before—I said "Johnny, I want you"—he said, "All right, sir, I know what you want me for; I suppose you will give me seven years for this"—I said, "I want you for that job in the City on Saturday night, being concerned with six other men in robbing an old gentleman"—I took him to the station, he was placed with six others of the same description, and when the prosecutor came in he immediately rushed at him—I had seen the prisoner about half-past one on the Sunday morning (this had occurred at half-past twelve, but I had then no knowledge of it), with three others, whom I know, at a coffee stall at the corner of Stamford Street, Waterloo Road—they were three well-dressed young fellows—they walked away and the prisoner remained behind—he knew me well—he said "Mr. Ward, are you not going to take Harry Nicholls?"—that was one of the three that walked away—I said "What for?"—he said "Haven't you heard?"—I said "No"—he said "I thought you knew all about it" (the coffee stall was about five minutes' walk from
the scene of the robbery)—I did not know what he was alluding to, I did not hear of the robbery till nine the following morning—at the station on the morning after he was identified and after the charge was made, he said "You have got me tight this time."
Cross-examined. I left you at the coffee-stall—I knew that the four men I saw there were thieves—the man I took to the station on the Sunday night was for another affair altogether.
WILLIAM WOODS ( Re-examined by the COURT). I have not been well since this attack—I was perfectly sober.
The Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "I am merely charged because I was seen at the coffee-stall."
GUILTY .
He then PLEADED GUILTY to a conviction of felony in March, 1881. Seven other convictions were proved against the prisoner.—Five 1882. Years' Penal Servitude and Twenty Strokes with the Cat.
[trialAccount t18840915-975]
975. WILLIAM WALLIKEK (28) , Stealing a watch and chain and 10 l. in money of Henry Clarkson, his master.
MR. BUCK Prosecuted.
HENRY CLARKSON . I live at 142, Newport Street, Lambeth Walk, and am a contractor—on Bank Holiday, 4th August, about 11 a.m., I went out with my wife and children, leaving a gold watch and chain of the value of about 20 l., in the drawers in my bedroom, and also about 10 l. in gold and silver in the same drawer; there was also a diamond ring there, that was afterwards found on the floor—I got home about 10 or half-past 10 at night and found the door of the room burst open—we had left it barred up and then gone out of the front door and locked it, leaving nobody in the house—the bedroom-door had been prised open—when we came back we lit the lamp and saw the ring on the floor—the watch and chain were gone and about 10 l. in gold and silver—this was on the ground floor—the prisoner has lodged with me two or three years, sleeping in a room on the ground floor, and was one of my carmen—on this morning he washed the trap and cleaned the harness before I went out—up to Bank Holiday night I think he had been in the habit of coming home regularly, on that night he did not come home—next night, Tuesday, I saw him with a woman at the Equestrian public-house at the side of the Surrey Theatre—he was three-parts drunk—he had not been to his work on the Tuesday—Constable Jupe was with me—I asked the prisoner "What have you done with the watch and chain?"—he made no answer, but stood there—I asked him about the money and told him what I had lost, and said "If you don't tell me where it is I shall lock you up"—he said nothing for a minute or two, but turned all manner of colours—I told him I had lost my misuses gold watch and chain that I gave 21 l. for and about 10 l. of my little children's gold and silver—I said nothing about the ring—he stood there for five minutes and made no answer, and then he said he did not know nothing about it and what he knew he was going to keep to himself—I gave him into custody—the prisoner knew all the ways of the house, as he had lived there—he had no business whatever in the bedroom or parlour.
JAMES HARMER . I am a carman in Mr. Clarkson's employment, and was so last Bank Holiday—I had just come back from my dinner and sat down in the yard when I saw the prisoner coming out from the door of the missus's bedroom into the yard—I did not see whether he had got anything—I was talking to another chap, Nickerboo, who works for Mr.
Clarkson—the prisoner could hear us; he said nothing to us—I asked him whether he was going to treat us, and we all went to the public-house and had something to drink, for which the prisoner paid—he changed half-a-crown for the first quart and a two-shilling piece for the second—he said nothing about the missus or how he had got the money—he asked me the time, and I told him, and he said he had to be at Camberwell at 1 o'clock—this visit to the Windmill was in working-hours, we were looking after the horses on the Bank Holiday—the prisoner had no business in the missus's bedroom—the wash-house which opens into the yard separates the missus's room from ours—you come into the wash-house, and then our room is on the left, and missus's room on the right—the door into the yard was open.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I can swear I saw you come out of that room, no one but you was there—the bedroom and parlour are separate rooms, leading into one another—there were three of us talking together, McCarthy was the third—we were all treated—you did not speak as you came out—I said "Are you going to treat us out of that shilling the governor gave you for washing his trap?"—you said we could come over and have some of the beer—the George is nearer than the Windmill—you did not give Boo twopence because he said he had no money.
Re-examined. He changed no shilling at the Windmill.
LOUIS LOCKWOOD . I am one of the prosecutor's carmen, and live next door—on Tuesday, 5th of August, I went to the prisoner at the police-station and said "Walliker, tell me where the missus's watch and chain is?"—he turned round and said to me "I know nothing about it, they cannot get nothing out of me; let them try what they like"—I asked him the same question again and he made the same reply—I did not ask him why he had not been to work on Tuesday morning; the policeman took me away then.
HENRY JUPE ( Detective Sergeant L). On the night of the 5th of August, at 10.30, I took the prisoner into custody at the Equestrian Tavern, Blackfriars Road—I said "You will be charged with stealing a watch and chain and 10 l. in money from your master"—he made no reply, but said "I will go"—I searched him at once and found 3 s. 6 d. in silver on him, and 7 1/2 d. in bronze—I should say he had been drinking heavily—he was three-parts drunk; he was smoking a cigar and had a woman with him—on the way to the station he said "I know nothing about the b——things"—the bedroom of this house is partitioned off from the parlour—the door could be easily burst open, there is only a small bolt—my recollection is that it was a half-glass door; the glass was whole—the wash-house door was left open for the men to get water—he would go through there to burst open the door.
The prisoner in his defence stated that on this day he came out of his own bedroom, and seeing the men in the yard treated them at their request out of the shilling his master had given him, and that on leaving them he met a suc-ession of friends, who continued treating him until he was given into custody.
GUILTY.— Judgment respited to give the prisoner an opportunity of giving some information as to the watch and chain.

977. THOMAS JONES (19) , Robbery with violence on David Vine and stealing his watch and chain.
MR. SAUNDERS Prosecuted; MR. RAVEN Defended.
DAVID VINE . I am a blacksmith, living at Kennington Cross—on the 23rd of August, about a quarter or 20 minutes to 11, I was in the Westminster Bridge Road, when the prisoner and another man came up to me—the prisoner came right up to my face and put his hand up and threw me over—I fancy I was kicked up, because I had a great lump on my leg—the prisoner broke my chain and took my watch; this is the other part of my chain—when I got up the prisoner was running down the street—I don't know where the other went to—I was lame before this with rheumatics—my watch was worth 5 l.—I have no doubt the prisoner is the man who took it.
Cross-examined. There was another man there; I did not recognise him as it was done so suddenly—I have not seen the prisoner before to my Knowledge—this was at the corner of Charles Street, on the public pavement—there was a lamp at the corner, and there I saw the two men standing as I crossed the street—I believe it was the other man that tripped me up—I shouldn't say the man who assaulted me had any whiskers—I am not very short-sighted; I can see better with glasses—I had my glasses on at the time—I did not notice that he was wearing a pin—I am positive he had a billycock hat on, I believe a black one—I believe the other had a black hat on, I could not swear to it—both of them had dark trousers on—I know nothing of the prisoner's character or that he holds a driver's licence.
By the COURT. Neither of them spoke—there were a lot of people looking on, no one interfered—for a week afterwards I suffered a great deal of pain; I had a lump as big as an egg under my knee, and my leg was grazed and my hands cut.
LOUISA STREET . I am the wife of Frederick Thomas Street, a colourgrinder, of 30, Westminster Bridge Road—about a quarter to 11 o'clock on 23rd August I heard cries of "Stop thief," and saw the prisouer running away in Charles Street—I am positive he is the man—I picked him out from about nine others at the police-station.
Cross-examined. I identified another man first, but after I went closer, and am quite sure now that this is the man—I was coming up Charles Street, which leads into Westminster Bridge Road—I saw only one man—no one was with me—I saw Mrs. Calloway there—I did not know her previously—the baker's wife was there too—I know she is the baker's wife because she came out of the shop, and I have seen her in the shop before—I don't know the baker's name—she is not here—I don't know whether Mr. Cadie, a shoemaker, lives in Charles Street, nor whether Mr. Calloway does—I said the prisoner had a slight moustache, not whiskers.
Re-examined. Mrs. Calloway came to the baker's corner that night that is why I recollect her so well.
MARY ANN CALLOWAY . I am the wife of William Calloway, a ladies' shoemaker, of 15, Charles Street—on the night of 23rd August I was standing; outside my door with my landlady—I heard a gentleman cry out, and then saw the prisoner trotting down Charles Street—I ran to the corner of the street by the baker's—the prisoner halted as he got to the corner, and then began to run very fast down Webber Row—I am quite sure the prisoner is he—he had a pin in his scarf then, and on the last remand I told him he had taken it out—I heard him tell Mr. Bridge he had the pin taken out of his scarf at the House of Detention—he had no whiskers; I could see marks of hair down his face, but since being in prison he has more of it—I went to the police-cell to identify him, but if was not light enough—when he came outside into the courtyard I told him he had taken the pin out of his scarf.
Cross-examined. Margaret Dougherty is my landlady—we were at the door—she says the prisoner is not the man, I say he is—it was between 9 and 10 o'clock—I had no means of knowing the proper time; it might have been a quarter to 11—he came from the top of Charles Street—I never said he came down Webber Street, that is a mistake—he came past my door and turned into Webber Row—our street is very dark—I was outside the door—I saw he had a pin in his scarf when I got to the baker's shop, which is well lighted—his whiskers have grown since I saw him; they were very slight then—he was placed with 11 man in such a dark cell that I could not see, and I did not identify him—when I came back on the Tuesday of the remand I talked to Mrs. Dougherty about it, and she said "Mrs. Calloway, it is not the man"—she was standing in the doorway with me.
Re-examined. She did not go to the corner where the lamp was.
FREDERICK GRAY ( Detective L). On Saturday night, 23rd August, I was in the Westminster Bridge Road, about 10 or a quarter to 10 o'clock—I saw the prisoner in the Oxford public-house with another man, who is still wanted for being concerned with him in this case—that is about 30 yards from where this occurred.
Cross-examined. There was no other acquaintance of mine there—there were three remands for Calloway's attendance—I gave evidence on the second or third, the third I think—I was in Court on the two previous occasions.
ALFRED WARD ( Sergeant L). On the morning of 26th August I apprehended the prison or in the Westminster Bridge Road, and told him he would be charged with being concerned with another not in custody in stealing a watch—he said "All right, I think you have made a mistake this time"—when the charge was read over to him he said "I suppose I must put up with the law."
Cross-examined. If it is on the deposition that I said he said, "I will put up with the law,"I made a mistake—at the police-court his words were,"I must put up with the law"—I did not take a note of what he said—I have made inquiries about his character—I endeavoured to see Mr. Deards, a builder, of Westminster Bridge Road; I called, he was at the seaside—I saw Mr. Warne, a cabinet-maker, in the presence of the prisoner's father—he told me the prisoner was apprenticed to him, and two years ago he was obliged to cancel his indentures; I swear that—the prisoner had a police conductor's licence, it has never been used;
they are only given to people of good character, and is generally obtained on the result of inquiries—I cannot say whether it is correct or not.
Re-examined. Mr. Warne said he had been obliged to cancel the prisoner's indentures two years ago and to discharge him, and he said to the prisoner's father, "You know, Mr. Temple, he has been keeping very indifferent company lately, I have seen him with" so-and-so, mentioning different names—the father said he would wash his hands of him.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MARGARET DOUGHERTY . I am single, and the landlady's daughter—I was standing with Mrs. Calloway at the door on this night, and saw a man come down the street running—I do not see that man here; the prisoner is not the man I am satisfied—he was by himself—he had a dark moustache and no whiskers—I have known the prisoner for three years by standing outside my door, but I have never spoken to him—I know nothing about the prisoner's character.
By the COURT. He was in the volunteers, and they used to assemble outside my window.
Cross-examined. Charles Street is badly lighted; there is one lamp, and one lamp over the baker's door—the lamp was next door to where I was standing, about a dozen yards off—I went to the corner with Mrs. Calloway; we all followed to the corner after the man ran away—he had got into the Waterloo Road by the time we got to the corner, and we did not see him, he had gone.
MARGARET TEMPLE . I am the prisoner's mother—the prisoner lived with us until quite recently—the last time he slept in our house was the night before Bank Holiday—he had a conductor's licence—his father in at work and not able to come here—I never heard his father say he would wash his hands of him—he shed bitter tears when we heard of it—I believe he was always an honest lad—after the statement of the sergeant that he was dismissed from his apprenticeship I saw Mr. Warne's manager, and Mr. Warne wrote this memorandum—he was in Mr. Deard's employ, and he entrusted him with 60 l. or 70 l.—he is out of town.
The Jury here intimated that they had made up their minds, and returned a verdict of
NOT GUILTY .

981. ELLEN SMITH (26) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted.
EMMA HILL . I am barmaid at the Winchester Arms, Southwark Street—on 21st August, about 2 o'clock, the prisoner came in and asked for a glass of ale, tendering a half-crown, which I tried in the tester and broke—I handed it to Mr. Kemp, the landlord—he told me to give it back to
the prisoner—I gave it to her, and asked her if she knew it was a bad one—she said she did not, and told me she had taken it at the pledge shop in Union Street; she told Mr. Kemp the same.
GEORGE KEMP . On 21st August, at 2 o'clock, the prisoner came in—I saw Emma Hill take some coin and put it in the tester; I stood by her and saw it break—I said to the barmaid "Go and ask her where she got it from"—she said "I got it from a pawnshop"—I said "It is a lie, show me your ticket"—she said "I won't"—I said "I shall send for a constable and give you in charge"—she said "You can do so, and then I won't show you the ticket"—I sent for a constable and gave her in charge—she said the pawnshop was in Union Street; that has been shut up for years.
ABBAHAM SURREY ( Policeman M 300). I was called to the Winchester Arms, and Mr. Kemp gave the prisoner into custody, saying the prisoner had come in for a glass of ale "Tendering me this half-crown," handing me a half-crown—I said to her "How did you come by it?"—she said "I got it at a pawnshop where I pawned my shawl"—I asked her for the ticket—she said "I shan't show it to you, now you know, do as you like"—she said the landlady had pawned the shawl in Union Street, Borough, and she had got the ticket—at the station in answer to the charge she said "The shawl was pawned in Union Street by the landlady, and she has got the ticket"—she gave her address at 5 l., Little Surrey Street—there is no pawnshop in Union Street, it has been done away with for two and a half years—before the Magistrate she said she had got the half-crown by selling a bunch of flowers to a gentleman, and afterwards told the old story that the landlady had pawned the shawl.
ELIZABETH REEVES . I live at 51, Little Surrey Street, Blackfriars Road—the prisoner lodged with me on 21st August—I have never pawned a shawl for her.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . This is a bad half-crown.
The prisoner in her defence stated that she gave the shawl to a woman to pawn, and that the woman gave her the half-crown and a penny, but that the woman could not now be found, as she had gone to the hop country.
GUILTY .
She then PLEADED GUILTY ** to a conviction of felony for having a galvanic battery for the manufacture of counterfeit coin in her possession at this Court in January, 1881, in the name of Ellen Raynor.— Five Years' Penal Servitude.
[trialAccount t18840915-982]
982. THOMAS CLIFFORD (29) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted.
GEORGE FRYER . I am barman at the Plasterers' Arms public-house, Seymour Street, Euston Square—on 16th May I was barman at the Winchester public-house, Southwark Bridge Road—on that day, at 11 p.m., the prisoner came in with another man and called for a pot of ale, tendering a two shilling piece in payment—I gave him 1 s. 8 d. change, and put the florin on a shelf by itself; there was no other florin there—my missis afterwards took it up and showed it to me; I found it was bad and broke it in two—the prisoners were in the house at the time—I cannot say if they saw it broken—I went to look for a constable—the prisoner went outside two or three minutes afterwards—they saw the
missis speaking and showing it to me—I stopped the prisoner as he was running away—a constable came up and I gave him into custody—he said he received the florin in his wages—I gave it to the constable—the prisoner was charged and discharged after a remand—as soon as he saw me going for a constable the prisoner came out of the door and began to run directly.
GEORGE LOMAS ( Policeman M 37). On 16th May I was on duty in Suffolk Street, Southwark Bridge Road, and saw the last witness holding the prisoner—I went up—Fryer said "I give this man into custody for passing a bad florin in the Winchester public-house, 201, Southwark Bridge Road"—he handed me a florin broken into these two pieces—the prisoner said "I have been working at St. Olave's Wharf, Tooley Street, to-day; I got the money there"—I searched him at the station and found 1 s. 6 d. in silver and eight coppers on him—he gave the name of Patrick Brennan—he was charged at Southwark Police-court and discharged.
ELIZABETH RAINER . I am single and keep a chandler's shop at 115, Broad Wall, Southwark—at half-past 9 on the evening of 21st July, the prisoner came in and asked for a pennyworth of bread and a pennyworth of cheese, offering me a shilling—I saw by its look it was bad—this ( produced) appears like it—I bent it in the tried—I said "Well, young man, this is No. 3 to-day, it is quite time this was stopped"—he said nothing, but pretended to be intoxicated by leaning against the place; he could not stand up—he spoke properly when he came in and asked for the bread and cheese—he said he had taken it where he was working—he gave the name of some docks, I cannot say what—I sent for a constable and gave him in charge—I had had other bad shillings tendered earlier on the same day, one at half-past 5 and one at eight, and I saw the prisoner outside the door when the one at 5.30 was offered me; and the man that offered it and the prisoner went away together, and seemed to be friends—I gave both the bad shillings to the constable.
Cross-examined. You did not appear to be under the influence of drink—I took the money up, turned my back to you, and came and said it was bad, and you said "I was not aware of it," and that you received it in your work.
HENRY ROUSE ( Policeman L 63). I was called to 115, Broad Walk—Elizabeth Rainer and the prisoner were in the shop—Miss Rainer produced this shilling and said she had had three bad shillings tendered that day—the first I do not know who broke—the second she gave to me and said "This was brought to me by a man about an hour ago"—and about the third she said "This man has given me this shilling; I tested it and found it was bad, and then sent for a constable"—the prisoner said "I did not know it was bad, I received it in wages last Saturday at St. Olave's Wharf, Bermondsey—I searched him in the shop and found a penny and an empty purse—I took him to the station—he was charged and said he had it at St. Olave's Wharf—he gave his address at St. Olave's Chambers, Tabard Street, a common lodging house.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These two shillings and this florin are all bad—the shillings belong to the same mould.
The prisoner in his defence stated that he was a dock labourer, and received the shilling in pay the night before, and had no idea that it was bad.
GUILTY **.— Fifteen Months' Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18840915-983]
983. WILLIAM WARD (18) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted.
FRANCES COWEN . I am shop woman to Mr. John Bennett, a baker, of 172 Tooley Street—at a quarter to nine on 29th July the prisoner came in and asked for a penny cake and tendered this half-crown in payment—I gave him 2 s. 5 d. change—I put the halt-crown in the till where there was one other which I am quite sure was good—it had been given me during the day—I put this in the tester, it broke—the week after I was fetched to Kennington Road Police-station and I picked out the prisoner from several others as the man that tendered the half-crown.
Cross-examined. I had not seen you before that night—I knew you again by your height.
GEORGE POCOCK . I am landlord of the Windsor Castle, West Street, Southwark—about 9.30 on 29th July my barmaid made a communication to me—I went to the bar and saw the prisoner there—I said "This is a bad half-crown," showing him the one—he said he got it from his master where he worked, and he produced a brass ticket with "E. D. Bennett & Co., Swan Lane Iron works, 58 morning," on it—he asked for the halt-crown back—I refused to give it—I asked him for the money for the ale which he had drunk—he walked outside the house—I followed and gave him into custody by the Spa Tavern, which is just by Spa Road, at the corner of which is the wall of some chocolate works, but some houses separate Spa Road from the wall—it was a dark night.
Cross-examined. You said you were very sorry, you did not know it was bad, and pulled out a ticket which I understood you got from your master.
Re-examined. I walked by his side the whole way from my house—I did not observe anything.
MARIAN SPENCER . "I am barmaid at the Windsor Castle—on 29th July the prisoner came and asked for a pint of ale, tendering a half-crown in payment—I cannot say if this is the one—I rang it on the counter, thought it was bad, and took it to Mr. Pocock and made a communication to him.
Cross-examined. I put it in the till, gave it to another lady, and she took it away and gave it to the governor—I did not hear you say you were very sorry.
GEORGE GRIFFEN ( Policeman M 36). I was called to the Windsor Castle and Mr. Pocock gave the prisoner into my custody for attempting to pass a bad half-crown—he said "I am the one"—I said "You will have to go to the station with me"—I took him to the station—he admitted passing it but did not know it was bad—he made two statements—one that it was given to him at the Borough Market and the other that he got it from his employer—he said he got it for pulling a barrow from Borough Market to some green for a man who gave him a half-crown and left him—on 2nd August I received these five half-crowns from Wm. Skinner—he did not keep the paper in which they were wrapped.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. You said you did not get it at your work but for pulling a barrow from Borough Market to Camberwell Green—but you made another statement as well, that you got it from your employer—they were two distinct statements.
WILLIAM SKINNER . I am a foreman of the chocolate works, 89, Spa Road—on 30th July at 7 o'clock a.m. I found five half-crowns wrapped
up in a strip of paper one and a half inches wide and 15 inches long lying about four or five feet from the wall. which is about 40 yards from the Spa Tavern, that is one side of the road and the chocolate works on the other—walking from West Street you would leave our works to the right—there were marks of coin on the paper—the coins were given to Griffen on the Saturday following.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These two half-crowns that were uttered and these five found under the wall are all bad—the two uttered and three of the five are all from the same mould.
Witness for the Defence.
JOSEPH COLLINS . I am a labourer, living at 11, Elpherton Street, Old Kent Road—I left you at half-past 8 p.m. on the night of 29th July—I was working for Mowlem and Burt at the back of St. George's Barracks—the job was stopped and I was out of work for a week, and I went down to your house on 22nd July about half-past 4 o'clock and asked you to come for a walk to St. George's Barracks to see if they had started—we went all rouud the park and round and back by the Strand—coming back you asked me to lend you a shilling, you said your hand was better and you were going to start work on Monday and would give it back to me on Saturday—I did so, and we went into a public-house at the corner of the Westminster Bridge Road—then we came out and I asked you to go to Mr. King's to get a trowel, and you left me then at about half-past 8 o'clock—you said you had been up to Walworth; you said you had earned something and had something to pay away, and you showed me a half-crown.
The prisoner in his defence stated that on this day he pulled a barrow to Camberwell Green and that the gentleman he did it for gave him a half-crown, that he then had dinner when Collins came in, and that they went for a walk till half-past 8 o'clock, and that he offered the half-crown without knowing it was bad.
GUILTY †— Six Months' Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18840915-984]
984. THOMAS GROVES (22) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin and having another in his possession.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted.
SARAH GOODALL . I am Thomas Henry Goodall's wife—we keep a general shop at 289, St. James's Road, Bermondsey—at 11 o'clock on 8th August the prisoner and another man came in—the other man asked for two bottles of ginger-beer and laid down this half-crown—I looked at it and said "This is a bad one"—I bent it—he said "Don't break it, I may get another in place of it, I took three yesterday for my week's wages, all the money I have in my possession is a penny"—I said "Never mind, that will do"—I gave him the half-crown back—he put it in his pocket and said to his friend "Come along," mentioning some name, and they both went out and up St. James's Road—I spoke to a neighbour about a quarter of an hour afterwards—the prisoner was brought back to my shop—I identified the half-crown at the station.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. You drank all the ginger-beer—when you came out of the shop with the detective you did not say "Did I give you this half-crown?"—I took a good half-crown out of my till and said "You can feel the difference between the two"—he felt them, and said "I think this," which was the one he had tendered, "is a bad half-crown."
ELIJAH TALBOT ( Policeman M 263). On the morning of the 8th of August, at 11 o'clock, I was on duty at St. James's Road—the prisoner and another man passed me walking quickly—I followed the prisoner; he crossed the road and ran when he saw Gaertner, another constable, speak to me—I followed him to the corner of the Old Kent Road, and then being out of breath Gaertner followed—the prisoner was brought back—as we ran he passed 324, St. James's Road.
JOHN GAERTNER ( Detective M). Talbot made a communication to me—I followed the prisoner who was running and arrested him in Peckham Park Road—I said "I am a police officer, what were you running for just now in St. James's Road?"—he replied "I have not been in the St. James's Road, neither have I been running"—I took him to Mrs. Goodhall's shop; she identified him as having been with another man who had attempted to pass a bad half-crown—I saw the prisoner pass 324—I produce seven counterfeit half-crowns given me by Mayhew; I showed them to Mrs. Goodhall, she picked out this one—it has signs of having been bent—it was then marked.
Cross-examined. I searched you at the station, there was nothing found on you—you told me that you did not know the other man, that he asked you to go and have a bottle of ginger-beer with him, haying met him close to the shop.
HENRY OLIVER . I am a clicker, and live at 324, St. James's Road—I have a little garden in front of my house—about half-past 11 on the 8th of August I saw a packet under the portico; I picked it up, it contained these three half-crowns wrapped up—on the grass I saw another packet, containing four half-crowns, also wrapped up in paper—I gave them to Mayhew.
FREDERICK MAYHEW ( Policeman M 261). Oliver made a communication to me, and handed me these seven half-crowns, which I took to the station and gave to Gaertner; four in one packet and three in another.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These seven half-crowns are all bad—the one that has been tested is not from the same mould as any of the others—there are no marks of the coins on the paper, which is like the professional paper used.
The Prisoners Statement before the Magistrate. "It was not me, sir, it was the man that asked me to have the ginger-beer. I don't know who he was."
The prisoner in his defence stated that he met the other man, who offered to treat him to some ginger-beer, and that he went into the shop with him, but that not liking the man's company after the affair about the half-crown, he started running at soon as he got outside the shop in order to get away from him.
SARAH GOODHALL ( Re-examined by the Court). The other man tendered the half-crown, and asked for the bottle of ginger-beer.
GUILTY *†— Eighteen Months' Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18840915-985]
985. ROBERT CUNNINGHAM DOYLE (53) and MARGARET HENDERSON (40) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted; MR. MONTAGU WILLIAMS Defended.
NESTORE ROSALI . I keep a confectioner's shop, at 117, Westminster Bridge Road—at 11 a.m. on 1st September I was serving with my wife; the female prisoner came in and asked for a quarter of a pound of ham; I was about to serve her, and then she said only two ounces—that came
to 2 d.—she tendered a half-crown, which my wife took and gave to me—I bent it in my teeth and made three teeth marks on the bottom of the date—2 s. 4 d. change was given her—she left the house—I followed her directly, stopped her, and asked her to be kind enough to give me another half-crown, because that was not good—she said she was quite certain that was good, and she came back to the shop, left the ham and change, and went away, saying she would bring the man who gave the change to her in three minute's time—in about three-quarters of an hour she was brought back by Rose—this is the coin I bent, with the marks of my teeth on it—she said she was certain the half-crown was good, and that it came from my place.
Cross-examined. My wife passed the half-crown to me and she gave the change—I tried it in my teeth and bent it—the prisoner was indoors when my wife gave it me.
MARIAN ROSATI . I am the last witness's wife—about 11 o'clock on 1st September the female prisoner came in, asked for some ham, and tendered a half-crown to me—I gave her change, putting the half-crown on the marbleslab at the back—I afterwards gave it to my husband—I gave the prisoner a two-shilling piece change and four coppers—she said the florin was a bad one—I took it back and gave her another—her half-crown was on the marble slab from the time I put it there to the time I gave it to my husband.
Cross-examined. I put the money on the slab—there were two florins and two sixpences there—I got my change from the back—she saw me put the half-crown at the back—there was no other half-crown there.
GERALD GRIFFIN . I am a barman at the Hercules, in Kennington Road—about half-past 10 o'clock I was serving; the male prisoner came in and asked for a half-quartern of fivepenny rum in a bottle—it came to 2 1/2 d.—he tendered a half-crown—I noticed it was already dented and rather bad, and I broke it in the tester—he said he had got it from a respectable house of business—my governor sent for a constable; he was given in charge—about half an hour afterwards I noticed the female prisoner in the other bar—I did not serve her—I gave the half-crown to the constable.
Cross-examined. The male prisoner insisted the half-crown was a good one, he asked the constable to take it to a jeweller's shop and have it tested—I believe it was sent from the police-station to a jeweller's, and the first jeweller said it was a good one—it was afterwards sent to two others.
HENRY BENNETT ( Policeman L 218). On 1st September I was called to the Hercules, and the male prisoner was given into my custody—I received these pieces from the last witness—the prisoner denied it was bad, and wanted to claim it back again—he is brother-in-law to the female prisoner.
WILLIAM ROSE ( Detective Sergeant L). On 1st September I was on duty at the Kennington Road Station when the male prisoner was brought in and charged with having attempted to pass a bad half-crown to Gerald Griffin—he was detained for me to make inquiries—he asked me to go to the Alma Coffee-house, ask for Miss Henderson, not for his wife, and she would tell me that she had received the half-crown from a ham and beef shop in the Westminster Bridge Road—I went to the Alma Coffee-house, saw the female prisoner, and she told me she went there for two ounces
of ham, handed a half-crown, and received a bad two-shilling piece and 4 d. change—I said she had better go to the shop with me; she did so—the proprietor, Nestore Rosati, said he recollected the female being in the shop during the morning, that she had been in for two ounces of ham, for which she put down a half-crown, which was afterwards detected to be bad, and that she ran out of the shop after that—on that I took her into custody, telling her it was a bad half-crown—the said, "No, it was a bad two-shilling piece you gave me"—she was afterwards taken to the station and charged.
SARAH ANN ROWLES . I keep a coffee-house at 5, Mount Street, Lambeth—on 30th August the two prisoners came to my house and slept there for two nights.
Cross-examined. The prisoner, his wife, and this woman.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These are four pieces of a bad half-crown.
Doyle's Statement before the Magistrate. "I believed it was a good half-crown. I got it at a respectable shop, and gave it to my sister.
NOT GUILTY
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, OCTOBER 20, 1884.
